IoT- Fundamentals and Challenges



Mohammad Maksuf Ul Haque1, Shazmeen Shamsi1, Khwaja M. Rafi2, Mohammad Sufian Badar3, 4, *
1 Department of Computer Science, Jamia Millia Islamia, New Delhi, India
2 Director, Mewat Engineering College, Palla, Distt. Nuh, Haryana, India
3 Department of Bioengineering, University of California, Riverside, CA, USA
4 Salfia Paramedical Institute Darbhanga, SPI Darbhanga, Bihar 846001, India

Abstract

The Internet of Things (IoT) is a revolutionary technology that aims at embedding everything we interact with or use with the capability to share and receive information over the internet. By everything, we mean all plausible things, from the refrigerator in your kitchen and washing machine to a soil moisture sensor on a farm and sprinklers in a stadium field. It has limitless applications in every field, including medical, industrial/manufacturing, security, etc., to name a few. This myriad of applications makes IoT a difficult concept to comprehend. To tackle this issue, we present this work which aims at defining the loosely bounded concept of IoT comprehensively with related examples and lesser technical jargon than usual. Slowly, we will dwell into technical aspects and real-life applications by talking about Wireless Sensory Networks (WSNs, a subset of IoT), Distributed Ledger Technology (or Blockchain), another revolutionary technology that is merging with IoT to give rise to an even more secure and robust range of applications. Then, we will discuss the third pillar, Artificial Intelligence (AI), that, when paired with the aforementioned systems, breathes life into the devices we interact with daily. Finally, we will weigh the challenges and prospects of the field.

Keywords: AI, Artificial Intelligence, Blockchain, Distributed Ledger Technology, IoT architecture, Internet of Things, IoT, IoT security, Machine Learning, ML.

* Corresponding author Mohammad Sufian Badar: Department of Bioengineering, University of California, Riverside, CA, USA; Tel: +1318 2783 121; E-mails: msbadar@engr.ucr.edu, director_academic@salfiainstitute.edu.in

1. INTRODUCTION

The chapter aims at explaining the fundamental concepts of the “Internet of Things” abbreviated as IoT. We humans, as intelligent beings, have always strived to make our lives easier, from the simplest things like a pressure cooker to state of the art self drivingcars [1] and UAVs used by the military for

surveillance [2], etc. IoT is yet another technological paradigm that is working towards making our lives easier and enriching our experiences and interactions with the things around us. The concept of IoT is a little difficult to grasp because of the diversity of things involved and the plethora of current and possible applications. Wikipedia defines IoT as “The Internet of things (IoT) is a system of interrelated computing devices, mechanical and digital machines provided with unique identifiers (UIDs) and the ability to transfer data over a network without requiring human-to-human or human-to-computer interaction.” [3]. While one can make some sense out of what it means, it is somewhat technical. The definition given by McKinsey, though small, seems more logical and makes the concept a little more understandable, “Sensors and actuators embedded in physical objects are linked through wired and wireless networks, often using the same Internet Protocol (IP) that connects the Internet” [4].

As mentioned earlier, the definitions in and of themselves are acceptable, yet they fall short of encompassing the depth and breadth of the concept. In this chapter, we will learn the fundamentals through various examples of what IoT is, but for now, let us talk about the other revolutionary technologies that go hand in hand with IoT to give us that enhanced interaction with devices around us. Artificial intelligence, abbreviated as “AI”, is a field of computer science and engineering that makes machines intelligent or, to be more precise, intelligent computer programs [5]. AI-enabled devices can by themselves automatically perform tasks that generally require human assistance or intervention. The definition given by Andrew Moore defines AI simply and concisely as, “Artificial intelligence is the science and engineering of making computers behave in ways that, until recently, we thought required human intelligence.”

This intelligence is achieved mostly through Machine Learning or ML and enables programs and software to learn and attain or mimic human-like intelligence, i.e., become Artificially Intelligent. One can say that ML is how computers learn to become intelligent or as Tom M. Mitchell said, “Machine learning is the study of computer algorithms that allow computer programs to automatically improve through experience.”

Next is another Technology whose popularity has erupted in recent years, the Distributed Ledger Technology, more popularly known as Blockchain, which is a way of maintaining records. Technically, Blockchain is a distributed, decentralized, public ledger [6]. At its most basic level, blockchain is just a chain of blocks, but not in the traditional sense of those words. When we say the words “block” and “chain” in this context, we are talking about digital information (the “block”) stored in public databases linked together (the “chain”). Both of these

technologies, i.e., AI and Blockchain are paving the way for more powerful, beneficial, and secure IoTsystems [7, 8].

The chapter discusses the history of IoT and then goes on to describe what IoT means through various examples and scenarios. After clearing the vast and vague concept of IoT, it talks about the fundamental principles, architecture, and frameworks of the technology. From there, it explores the current challenges in this field like scalability and security [9] and what can be done about it. In the end, this chapter leaves the reader with hints of what and where the future of IoT might be.

The chapter aims at developing a clear understanding of IoT. It provides the reader with a perspective regarding the emergence of IoT, how it came to be what it is now, and then it talks about IoT as in the real world around us, so that the omnipresence of these devices embedded with the internet can be understood more clearly. By discussing the fundamentals, architecture, etc., it familiarizes the reader with the technicalities involved in IoT and the requirements and considerations to be paid attention to while developing an IoT solution. It then enriches the reader with the current challenges of the field to give an understanding of what we lack in. At last, with prospects about the expansion of IoT, we have to give the reader something to think about and work on if they want to pursue a career in the field of IoT.

2. HISTORY OF IOT

In the world of technology, one would consider the IoT as something old. The thought or vision of machines communicating with one another can be found in the works of people like Nikola Tesla’s interview with Colliers in 1926 [10] and literature from the early 1800s. In a way, machines can be said to be in contact since the first Telegraph in the mid-1800s. In 1900, the first Voice over Radio transmission (Wireless telegraphy), and many more inventions and innovations in the field of communication and electronics followed, laying the foundation for IoT. Amidst all the rapid advancements, the development of computers began in the 1950s.

The Internet, a significant component of the IoT, started as part of a military defense project called DARPA in 1962 and evolved into the ARPANET in 1969. Commercial service providers started supporting the public use of ARPANET around the 1980s, which allowed the modern Internet to come into existence. Internet and communication technologies were quintessential for IoT’s development. IPV6’s decision to increase address space was another important component in developing a functional IoT.

2.1. Realizing the Concept

The term IoT was coined in 1999 by Kevin Ashton, but the technology came into existence even before that. One of the first examples was a Coca-Cola machine from the early 1980s [11], located at the Carnegie Mellon University. Programmers there connected a refrigerator to sensors and connected it to the internet, so they could keep tabs on the drinks in it, preventing them from futile trips to the vending machine. Then came the toaster presented by John Romkey at Interop in 1990 [12]. It was a toaster connected to the internet that could be turned on and off from a computer. There are some pieces of evidence of the existence of elevators that could be wirelessly controlled in 1979 [13], but it isn’t very clear, so the Cola vending machine is considered the first IoT implementation.

The first corporate attention to IoT was from LG when they announced the first internet refrigerator back in 2000. From there, IoT advanced rapidly and started appearing in mainstream media like The Guardian [14], Scientific American, etc. around 2003. UN's International Telecommunications Union, ITU, published its first report on the topic “A new dimension has been added to the world of information and communication technologies (ICTs): from any time, any place connectivity for anyone, we will now have connectivity for anything. Connections will multiply and create an entirely new dynamic network of networks – an Internet of Things” in the year 2005. IoT rose in recognition and potential from their onwards.

By 2013, IoT evolved into a system engaging many different technologies, like wireless communication, micro-electromechanical systems (MEMS), and embedded systems. The support of IoT nowadays is present in nearly all automation fields from home to industry, present-day sensor networks, control systems, etc.

3. INTERNET OF THINGS

3.1. Meaning of IoT

Let’s think about our phones today. What can they do? They can call, text, play songs, movies, books, etc. How do they carry out these tasks? By using the internet, of course. With no internet, they have limited capabilities. Equipping devices with internet capabilities comes with many benefits. This can be clearly seen in our own devices like smartphones, laptops, and tablets. The same increment in potential upon connection to the internet is also applicable to everything else, from the appliances you own to electrical devices around you, or some other asset of yours, literally everything. The central idea or concept of the IoT is simple i.e. to take everything and connect it to the internet [15]. All the muddle about IoT is basically due to the fact that its definition and idea are so broad and loosely bound, that it gives rise to multitudes of possibilities and examples, making the concept of the IoT difficult to grasp. To understand this further, we will see why connecting things to the Internet matters.

3.2. Importance of IoT

Devices or things connected to the internet gain the ability to receive or send information, and in some cases, do both. With this comes the title of being smart, for example, smartphones, smart TVs, smart homes, etc. By connecting them to the internet, these devices exceed their limitations in terms of capabilities, functionalities, and experience. Let's consider our smartphones again. Nowadays, we can listen to any song of our choice on our phones, not because we have them all stored in our phones, as we cannot possibly store all the songs available on our phones. This is the part where the internet plays a crucial role. How does it work? Every song in the world is stored somewhere on some device that can send information (in this case a song) when asked for by another device like our own phones. It uses the information that the internet provides, and hence we can listen to the song of our choice. This exchange of information is the ability that the internet provides us with. This enables even small devices like our phones to have access and utility of high storage capacity devices or even a supercomputer with just a touch. That is the power that comes with connecting our devices to the Internet. All the devices being connected to the internet can be broadly classified into three categories, and they work together to deliver potential beyond the scope of one single device. The categories are:

  • Things that Collect and Send Information
  • Things that Receive Information and then Act on it
  • Things that can do both

3.2.1. Things that Collect and Send Information

Just like we have sensory organs that can sense heat, touch, motion, smell, sound, light, etc., and send this information to our brain for processing, there are devices that can collect information from our surroundings called sensors. They collect data from our surroundings like light, wind, speed, temperature, etc. If they are connected to some processing unit, they can send it there for utilization. Providing the sensors with the capability to exchange information over the internet makes them more powerful tools, as the data that was earlier available to the locality of the device can now be accessed from anywhere in real-time and can be processed by powerful computing devices to draw out useful conclusions.

3.2.2. Things that Receive Information and then Act on it

We all have used devices or appliances that start to work or perform a given task when we give them a signal. For example, to change the temperature in refrigerators we usually turn the knob or press the buttons to do so. The commands can be as simple as an on and off button, or as complex as Mozart's melody playing through a speaker. Now, what happens when you connect these things to the internet? The accessibility of the functions or actions of the device skyrockets. You can now change the temperature in your refrigerator while watching TV and sitting on your couch in the living room, you can turn off the lights of another room from a different room or even outside your home. This expansion of accessibility and experience is another powerful tool that comes with connecting things to the Internet.

3.2.3. Things that Can Do Both

These devices are the most powerful ones amongst all IoT devices, as they offer you both utility and accessibility. They can collect information, send it to devices that need it, and receive information and act on it. This is where the real potential of IoT can be seen blooming. Let us now consider through an example the first two points individually and then, later on, we can merge them to understand how the things with the capabilities of both sending and receiving information can enrich our lives and make it easier.

For this example, let’s say IoT-enabled sensors that can detect moisture percentage in the soil are installed by a farmer on his farm, which sends data directly to his home computer or mobile informing him about the moisture percentage in the soil. Now the farmer can irrigate his fields on time. This also prevents him from over and under irrigation, therefore saving him money as well. Next, he installed an automatic irrigation system on his farm. If he connects it to the internet, then he can irrigate his crops at any time. The first one was an example of things that collect data and send it, while the second one was an example of things that receive input data and act on it. Now, moving further, he can connect both the moisture sensors and the irrigation system to the internet, and enable them to communicate with each other. To do that he just needs to set the threshold of moisture percentage. If the moisture level of the soil falls below that given threshold, the sensor automatically sends him the data and at the same time signals the automatic irrigation system to start irrigation. This way even at night time or when the farmer is busy, he doesn’t have to worry about irrigating the fields if the moisture percentage falls below a certain level because the system will do it by itself as and when necessary. The farmer can also take it a step further by making use of the weather reports. He just needs to check the weather reports about the rain. As soon as the sensor detects a low moisture percentage, it will first look up the weather report. If there is a probability of rain in the next few hours, then it will just wait for the rain. If the prediction fails, then the irrigation system will start after a few hours. This way, the farmer will save on irrigation costs, electricity, and effort. Here, the whole system of moisture sensors, auto-irrigation system, and connection to weather prediction algorithms form this network of devices that can both send and receive information, collect data, and act upon it, thereby making our work easier than before [16].

3.3. Scope of IoT: Applications and Examples

The Internet of Things has the capability to aid every organization, of whatever line of work they may be in. It has the ability and tools to add value to the work of the organization and expand it in a previously unthinkable manner. If we keep on connecting previously existing devices and things to the internet and add new devices with capabilities tailored for IoT, some of the results or outputs will seem like magic. We can see IoT in action around us in our daily lives too. For example, we have all used or know someone who uses smartwatches or smart home devices like smart bulbs or fans. These smartwatches give us insights into our daily activities and health, and they do so by collecting physical data about how much we walk, our heart rate, our body temperature, etc. We can also control our smart appliances even when we are away from home, as long as they are connected to the internet. This enhances the experience and drastically increases the accessibility of the devices. For example, a little prior to arriving at our homes we can turn on the air conditioner from our smartphones, or switch on the geyser from our couches before taking a bath, so we don't have to wait for the water to heat up when we get in. Over time, learning from experiences (through ML), these devices can start doing some of these tasks by themselves so you won’t have to. While talking about the benefits of IoT systems, we should keep in mind that they do one or more of the following things: increasing efficiency, improving health and public safety, and enhancing user experience.

3.3.1. Increasing Efficiency

An increase in efficiency means that we get the same results with less work or better results with the same amount of work. Even though inefficiency on an individual level can be overlooked at times, it is quintessential for organizations or industries to increase efficiency. Because it directly affects investment and revenue, increasing efficiency becomes very important [17]. For example,

  • In factories, bottlenecks in the workflow can be identified by embedding sensors in the equipment. Addressing those bottlenecks can help increase the output, therefore increasing efficiency.
  • Instead of scheduled maintenance, industries can embed equipment with sensors or sensing machinery and analytics which can predict the exact maintenance period [18]. In this way, the equipment’s life is increased. Unnecessary servicing is avoided, therefore lowering expenditure. Real-time monitoring reduces the chances of accidents by preventing malfunctions.

3.3.2. Improved Health and Safety

This aspect influences all levels of human society, from individuals to organizations and even Governments [19]. Advanced sensory devices and real-time monitoring play an important role here. For example,

  • Minute changes in the physiological conditions of patients can be monitored continuously, notifying doctors in real-time of any concerning anomalies. Detection of a heart attack prior to any discernible symptom or pain is possible through well-trained algorithms [20].
  • Natural disasters like cyclones or tsunamis can be predicted by using IoT sensors and connected analytical devices that can monitor and decipher the minute changes in the environment(pressure, humidity, wind speed, etc.) before these cataclysmic events [21]. This ensures a considerable reduction in damage to life and property as warnings can then be issued well in time for evacuation and rescue.

3.3.3. Enhancing Experience

Until now, most of our devices, if not all, work on the basis of passive input. With the advent of the IoT, along with Machine Learning, this status quo is changing. We are using our devices more than ever before, and our devices are not just responding, but rather, interacting with us and sometimes even further suggesting things or performing tasks on their own. This enhances the user experience and makes us feel more comfortable while interacting with other devices. It might make us feel a bit uneasy knowing that our devices can act without permission, but the reality is that they learn from our habits over time by observing our activities, what are our preferences, or time of usage, etc [22, 23]. For example, a smart home equipped with smart air conditioners or thermostats can change the temperature of the room based on how we usually keep it at night or day or at a specific time. Further, it can adjust the temperature depending upon the weather outside. This helps reduce energy consumption as well.

Looking at all of this, we can say that ML and IoT are both on their own powerful technologies, and their coalition opens to us an array of new opportunities to grow and enhance our lives and our interaction with technology.

4. FUNDAMENTALS OF IOT

Now that we’ve understood what IoT does, let’s see how it works.

4.1. IoT Device Architecture

There are four layers in the device architecture [24].

  • The base layer consists of IoT devices and includes all the components like sensors with the ability to sense, compute, and connect other devices.
  • The second layer, which is the IoT gateway or aggregation layer, significantly aggregates data from various sensors.
  • The third layer is based on the cloud. It is called the processing engine or event processing layer. It has numerous algorithms and data processing elements that are ultimately displayed on a dashboard, this layer processes the data obtained from the sensor layer [25].
  • The last layer is called the application layer or the API management layer. It acts as an interface between a third-party application and the infrastructure.

The entire landscape is supported by device managers and identity and access managers, which are useful for the security of the architecture.

4.2. IoT Reference Architecture

There are various layers in IoT reference architecture [26]:

  • Device layer: The device layer is the main component, where there are various devices like sensors that are interconnected. Examples include Bluetooth connected via mobile phone and Zigbee via Zigbee gateway. The other devices include the Raspberry pi that is connected to the ethernet via wi-fi. This is directly connected to the communication layers which are a part of the second layer.
  • Communication layer: The communication layer or gateway layer has rest protocols and other application-level protocols. Both layers are tightly coupled and generate an enormous amount of data.
  • The bus layer: Also known as the aggregation layer, it forms a bridge between the data and the communication layer for the sensors. It is an important layer for three reasons
  • It supports an HTTP server hand or an MQTT broker
  • It aggregates and combines communications via a gateway
  • It bridges and transforms data between different protocols
  • The event processing and analytics layer: It drives data and provides the transformation of the data generated. It provides the ability to do event processing. The data is stored in the database.
  • The client layer: This layer is used to create a web-based engine to interact with external APIs. This can be fed into the API management systems. This layer helps create a dashboard and provides a view of the analytics event processing. This layer helps communicate with systems outside the network using the machine-to-machine communication.

So far, we have seen the comprehensive IoT architecture with various components, rule engines, interfaces, and security systems embedded in it.

Now, Cross-functional architecture is possible using the device manager. It provides a single platform for remote management [27, 28]. The device manager communicates with devices through set protocols, device management uses device management agents, and is responsible for the remote management of software. The identity layer has cybersecurity capabilities, including policy control and OAuth2 token insurance. Other capabilities include Identity services XACML/PDP and directory of users e.g.. LDAP.

There are many frameworks for IoT setup, but the most common is ISO 30141. It provides commonly used vocabulary, reusable designs, and best practices for any developer to design an application. It also has many secure application standards that derive maximum benefit for the organization and reduces risks.

4.3. IoT Standardization and Design Considerations

There are several IoT standards that are evolving. Some of the key ones are [29].

  • M2M is a machine-to-machine service layer that can be embedded in hardware and software to connect devices.
  • Contiki is an open-source operating system for low-cost low-power IoT microcontrollers.
  • Light OS, a UNIX-like operating system, for wireless sensor networks.
  • Random phase multiple access is a proprietary standard for connecting IoT objects.
  • Sig Fox, a proprietary low power, low throughput technology for IoT and M2M communications.

4.4. IoT Interoperability Challenges

IoT maturity comes with several challenges, specifically about interoperability and interfacing [30]. Some of the prominent reasons are:

  • Coexistence of multifarious systems, devices, sensors, equipment, etc. that interchange location-time dependent information in varied data formats, languages, data models, constructs, data quality, and complex interrelationships.
  • Multivision systems are designed by manufacturers over time for varied application domains, making formulations of global agreements and commonly accepted specifications difficult.
  • New things are introduced that support new unanticipated structures and protocols.
  • Existence of low-power devices that need to exchange data over lousy networks and may have minimal likelihood or accessibility for a power recharge in months or years.

When choosing an IoT solution, several factors are considered, like its wireless capability, functionality, interoperability, secure storage, immediate boot capacity, device categorization, bandwidth, cryptographic control, and power management. The design considerations should be a mix of the estimated average of all these components and index to balance the user requirements. A dispute-resolution mechanism is also needed in case of failure in the long run.

4.5. IoT Device Architecture Network and Cloud

The IoT architecture is a combination of things, device platforms, and sensors with data. There are four stages of integrating the different IoTprocesses [31],

  • Stage one: networked things like wireless sensors and actuators.
  • Stage two: sensor data aggregation systems and analog to digital data conversion.
  • Stage three: the appearance of edge IT systems.
  • Stage four: analysis management and storage of data.

As these stages are evolving, the devices, the network, and the cloud application must be leveled equally in the ecosystem for better stability and security.

Stage 1 of an IoT architecture consists of networked things, usually wireless sensors and actuators. Stage 2 has internet gateways and data acquisition. That includes sensor data aggregation systems and analog to digital conversion. Stage 3 edge IT systems pre-process the data before it moves to the data center or cloud. Stage 4 data center and cloud is where the data is analyzed, managed, and stored on traditional back-end data center systems. Fundamentally, we need to have a functional, scalable, and maintainable architecture. If these are not supported then architecture is not useful.

Now, let's look at the three architecture areas of IoT:

  • One, the client-side IoT device layer
  • Two, operators on the server-side IoT gateway layer
  • Three, a pathway for connecting clients and operators, IoT platform layer.

These three layers interact with each other on a data synchronization front. The path would generate more data from various applications. The feasibility of the layers depends on their application.

4.6. Centralized and Decentralized IoT Architectures

The centralized architecture is a hub and is managed from one point, whereas the decentralized one is based on the use case; they do not help in industrial IoT solutions. The centralized architecture is associated with cloud architectures, in which a central hub provides a series of back-end services to smart devices. In the decentralized architecture, many scenarios require continuous communication between smart devices in an IoT topology without the need for an essential hub. The centralized systems help with event processing and decentralized systems operate more peer-to-peer messaging. Decentralized auditing is one of the essential features of decentralized architecture [32].

5. CHALLENGES

5.1. Security

The issue of security has been an obstacle in the large-scale deployment of IoT systems. Generally, IoT devices are more prone to Distributed Denial of Service (DDoS) attacks because of security vulnerabilities. In these attacks, the central server is bombarded with a huge volume of data requests simultaneously through multiple compromised computers, which causes the service to fail. Several DDoS attacks in recent years have disrupted organizations and individuals. Unsecured IoT devices provide an easy target for cyber-criminals to exploit the weak security protection to hack them into launching DDoS attacks. An overview of some of the attacks on different levels as described by Arora et al. is given in (Fig. 1).

Fig. (1))
Types of attacks on networks of Iot.

5.2. Customer Privacy

Sensors and cameras are becoming more and more commonplace these days, from our homes to public spaces. Adding to that we, the consumers, have less and less knowledge of the information being collected, and there is no way to avoid it.

The corporate use of personal data has become an everyday thing, and to some people’s displeasure, companies are making millions of dollars from selling our data. From habits to likings to whereabouts, there is a record of almost everything we do these days. According to a survey by CISCO in 2019, 84% of the people said they care about their privacy and the privacy of other members of society and 80% are willing to act to protect it [33].

There are mainly three privacy concerns in IoT:

  • Privacy in the device: It means that on the user end, the devices that record information like sensors or cameras are a threat to user privacy because the information they record can be leaked and misused. One way to tackle this is to have these devices made from reliable sources that follow strict standards of security and privacy.
  • Privacy during communication: Again, the confidentiality of data is a concern while transferring or transmitting data over the network. There are many ways through which people can tap into these communication channels and extract the information without authorization. Protocols that are designed for secure communications would help tackle this issue.
  • Privacy in storage: As it is evident that all the data collected through sensors for cameras or other devices is stored on a central storage server or cloud, it is an easy target to steal massive amounts of private user data. One way to resolve this would be the use of highly secure encryption algorithms while storing the data.

As of now, privacy has been a very black and white issue. The companies put up a front that users agreed to their privacy policy. In reality, very few of us ever read those privacy policies we click yes to, and even if someone does it is usually written in such convoluted fashion that it is hard to understand that we are being exploited. These things have fueled the discussion about privacy quite a bit.

5.3. Scalability

The growing number of devices creates a bottleneck, as the IoT of the present-day is a centralized system, and processes like authentication, authorization, and connection with different nodes with a large number of devices are bound to cause problems. One way out of this would be to make bigger investments to set up more powerful servers with higher computing capabilities. However, if that server faces some issue, the whole system crashes.

The number of IoT devices is steadily growing. A new forecast from the International Data Corporation (IDC) estimates that there will be 41.6 billion IoT devices, or “things,” generating 79.4 zettabytes (ZB) of data in 2025 [34]. CISCO estimates that 5 quintillion bytes of data will be produced every day from IoTdevices [35]. With such staggering numbers, one can easily say that IoT will be an integral part of our coming lives and that makes the above concerns even graver.

Cybersecurity researchers and experts have written reports showing their concerns about security loopholes in IoT systems, bringing to light the vulnerabilities of interconnected devices that can be exploited by hackers to wreak havoc. The Federal Trade Commission is also taking it seriously, trying to understand the ways to safeguard privacy in these new interconnected devices and to ensure that proper new regulations and protocols are derived and followed in the devices of the coming age. For example, with the advent of driverless vehicles or self-driving cars, etc., the threat of them being hijacked or tapping in their wi-fi or other connection services is a real problem the automotive industry has to tackle. Security risks cannot be overlooked while creating new ways to connect things to IoT, as every new method comes with its own risks and loopholes.

6. FUTURE

IoT can be visualized as a technology that is potentially limitless and can be merged with every possible technology in almost all sectors one can think of. Today, investments in AI-based IoT companies and devices are constantly on the rise owing to the fact that many sectors want to benefit from it. Innumerous companies now want to explore AI and IoT, starting from drafting a strategy to evaluating new projects or impr oving existing ones.

There have been several indicators of this major shift in the market:

  • The capital funding of AI-based IoT start-ups is rapidly growing. This can be understood from the statistical data as per which in the first eight months of 2017, the start-ups raised $705 million [36].
  • Acquisitions of AI-focused IoT start-ups are on the rise. This can be seen in the data, according to which it was 11 in 2015, 24 in 2016, and 21 in only the first 8 months of 2017 [37].
  • The established vendors of IoT platforms like Amazon [38], GE [39], IBM [40], Microsoft [41], etc. are integrating AI capabilities.
  • Large organizations have already started to leverage the potency of AI with IoT to operate more efficiently, thereby delivering better offerings [42, 43].
  • Gartner, a global research and advisory firm predicts that by 2022, more than 80 percent of IoT projects will include an AI component [44].

IoT paired with AI, ML, and Blockchain helps us understand the trends in the market and where all the big companies like SHELL [45], WALMART [46], etc. are going. What we see today is just a sliver of the full scope. The potential will only be realized with time. Talking about security, in organizations consisting of thousands of employees, AI is used to determine the regular access patterns and the roles of different employees, providing insights for future job layouts, and potentially detecting suspicious activities. Facial recognition technology has made massive leaps and bounds in the last few years alone. It has probably never been easier, with a camera on nearly every computer and the smartphones present today, to glean information from consumer reactions to products and marketing. We could use the example of Facebook’s auto-tagging here, which most people are familiar with.

In the future, it will be nearly impossible to find IoT systems that don’t utilize AI services. AI will support an “all effective” IoT effort, and without AI, data from the deployments will have “limited value”. We can see that it has nearly come to that as of today. Many IoT vendors are offering at least basic AI support. Companies across the globe in all major industries are already benefiting from AI in their IoT deployments. It is expected that in the future these AI-enabled IoT devices will make lives convenient for us to such an extent that automation will become a reality and technologies like automated house cleaning, AI-enabled smart locks, self-driving cars, augmented and virtual reality, smart cities, Blockchain generated cryptocurrencies, and all other beneficial practical examples of present-day sci-fi technology will become the new normal.

7. CONCLUSION

As is clear from the above discussion, the Internet of Things is an omnipresent technology even in the current scenario and will keep growing exponentially. The advantages by far outnumber the cons of the technology, but the infrastructure to integrate it into our daily life to make IoT a daily commodity and an essential service is yet to be completely manifested. There are several limits currently holding back the best of these devices. Though IoT devices are pretty common these days, as we have seen, the full utilization of the potential of this technology has not yet been realized. There are many reasons for it, from security concerns like attacks from adversaries to privacy concerns of data theft or misuse of data, even by the companies that are collecting them, and the issue of scalability as the larger-scale implementation of IoT will be needed to make industry and businesses profitable. However, there are several restrictions with the current technologies. Several technologies like Artificial Intelligence and Distributed Ledger etc., are being used to tackle these issues. Every day, we see new developments being made and milestones being reached to further enhance the usage and utilization of IoT. There is a lot to be done and a lot of investment and efforts are required to overcome these hurdles. However, it is all worth it if we consider the enormous benefits IoT brings to the table, from improving livelihoods to enriching interactive experiences with devices and surroundings. It even plays a role in sustainable development through smart cities, smart farming, and other services. Hopefully, by reading the above chapter and discussions, the reader now has a clear understanding of the emergence of IoT and its fundamentals like its architecture, frameworks, etc., and also the overview of the challenges faced by the technology and possible solutions and prospects of it. This leaves the reader with the potential to expand their knowledge on the aspects of IoT that interests them and work their way forward for improvement in technology and the society we live in.

IoT Based Energy Conservation of A Smart Home



Somya Goyal1, *, Sudhir K. Sharma2
1 Manipal University Jaipur, Jaipur, Rajasthan and Guru Jambheshwar University of Science and Technology, Hisar, India
2 Institute of Information Technology and Management, Janakpuri, New Delhi, India

Abstract

Smart home is one of the most attractive application of Industry 4.0. This work proposes a smart system to conserve energy in smart home implementation using wireless sensor networks (WSNs). The proposed work is aimed to lessen the immaterial consumption of electricity in a smart home. The home is supplied with Arduino, sensors, and connected WSN. The model observes the movement within the house premises and controls the power cutoff decided by the control center connected via WSNs. To detect the motion, passive infrared (PIR) sensors are deployed. As the PIR sensor detects the motion into the room, the lights are automatically turned on and then, if no motion is observed after a threshold time, the lights are automatically switched off. In this manner, the wastage of electricity can be controlled. Experimental results show that the consumption of power can be decreased by at the rate of 40% with the proposed model in comparison to the situation in absence of any such smart control. The validation of proposed model shows that the proposed system is feasible, effective, and not so expensive to implement.

Keywords: Energy conservation, Passive infrared sensor (PIR), Smart homes, Wireless sensor networks (WSNs).

A smart home is a term utilized for a home that is outfitted with electrical and electronic gadgets being worked on utilizing distant regulators (smartphones and so on). the WSN. The attraction for home mechanization frameworks in homes and workplaces is consistently expanding. In 2013, the value of the market business for home automation was approximately US$5.77 billion, and in 2020, it reaches US$12.81 billion [1]. The composition of smart home systems is as follows:

i) Input the readings from the environment via sensors – smoke sensors, moisture sensor, temperature sensors, ii) Constant observation and input from the sensor for fire detection, CCTV footage analysis, iii) Automated controlling feature – automated control of lights, fan, A/C, automated water sprinklers in case of fire, fire alarms and iv) AI and Logic – smart security mechanisms.

4. Architecture & Framework For Smart Home Environment

A standard framework is desirable when multiple heterogeneous devices are required to perform jointly in Smart Home Environment (SHE) in a collaborative manner to save energy. Energy-efficient architecture in SHEs is required to analyze the habit patterns and environmental factors to automate smart home appliances. It will bring interoperability among the heterogeneous devices which are developed to work in isolation. This architectural framework is based on the centralized model in Fig. (6).

Fig. (5))
Sample SESAME system.
Fig. (6))
Smart home environment-architectural framework.

The heart of this centralized framework is the ‘Central smart management system’. This Central management system consists of two sub-components - Central Control Unit (CCU) and Central dedicated software (CDS). The CCU is responsible for the data aggregation and decision-making core component. It deals with all entities involved within the system. It is responsible for all real-time information processing and allows decision-making support. It also executes algorithms to forecast energy consumption in SHEs, and also in the reduction of energy consumption, it plays a key role.

The communication among the various components of this architecture is ensured by specific peripheral devices. For example- the communication between CMS and home appliance say refrigerator. Such a specific peripheral is known as a ‘smart plug’. It is so-called as it adds on the intelligence to the ordinary appliance and puts it onto the connected network (Internet). The smart plugs also run energy-controlling tasks on the appliances. It can monitor the energy utilization design alongside the status of the machine. Extra energy-control offices are forced by 'brilliant box'. These are equipped for checking the machines alongside controlling the actuation and de-enactment of the gadgets. This engineering holds a significant component named 'UI' to permit its connection with the clients through committed Web-based applications and additionally versatile applications.

The engineering incorporates a group of sensors to gather information, shrewd attachments, and keen boxes to run astute rationale. After the information is gathered, it is kept in the information vault. From this archive, information is brought and handled to decide the enactment/de-initiation of apparatus, evaluate the energy utilization examples of occupants, reconnaissance, and so on.

5. Implementation of Smart Home Using Zigbee

This part presents the applications of SHE with Zigbee. The goal is to give energy to the executives in a brilliant home with ease. It gives the far-off administration and control of the family machines. It helps in ceaseless and steady observing of homes from distant areas through IoT to decrease energy utilization. It permits rescheduling the occupant's working time according to the energy request. It includes a PIR sensor to computerize the actuation/de-initiation of lights naturally to moderate the energy.

5.1. Smart Home Control System

The framework includes 3 significant modules: the Data assortment module, the Communication module, and the Device Controlling module, as shown in Fig. (8).

Fig. (8))
Implementation for smart home.

Information assortment is executed by means of Wireless Sensor Networks. The collected information acquired with the assistance of Arduino Uno, is shipped off the administration station by means of a Zigbee handset.

The administration module deciphers the assembled information to check the status of home machines, it screens and computerizes them according to prerequisites or the ecological components.

Notwithstanding controlling the machines according to the information accumulated and design watched, this usage introduces a movement sensor, for example, the PIR sensor- Passive Infrared Sensor. It is proficient to detect the presence of humans inside the room and as indicated by that, it naturally turns the light and fan ON. At the point when a human leaves the room, its faculties and turn the light and fan OFF consequently. Further, the Light Sensor, LDR is associated with the sense of sunshine and murkiness to mechanize the brightening. A temperature sensor is used to detect the temperature information, to robotize the turning ON/OFF of the fan. This execution utilizes 3 sensors to detect the presence of any human and the state of need of light and fan. In view of the information gathered, sent by means of Arduino to the focal regulator (PC), initiation/de-actuation is controlled distantly.

5.2. Two Major Functions

5.2.1. Framework Controlling Lights

This framework performs two functions- 1) detection of availability of light in the room and 2) movement of human as some activity in the room. To achieve these functions, light-dependent resistors (LDR) and PIR sensors are embedded in the system respectively (shown in Fig. (9)). This control framework automatically turns the lights on depending upon the situation of non-availability of light and movement by humans and vice versa. In this way, as per the need for lights in the room, this control executes automated switching on or off the lights.

5.2.2. Framework Controlling Fan

This framework performs two functions- 1) detection of the temperature of the room and 2) detection of movement in the room by some humans. To perform these two functions, it utilizes a sensor to sense the temperature readings and a PIR sensor to detect movement. It turns the fans on in the situation when the temperature is high and there is some movement in the room and vice-versa. This automated control is capable to handle the over-consumption of energy specifically the wastage of energy.

The entire system is dedicated to automating the switching on the light/fan as per the requirement. It instantly switches off the devices as they are no longer needed. Hence, the wastage of energy meanwhile is minimized.

Fig. (9))
PIR sensor detection.

This section practically presented the implementation of automated illumination of light for smart homes employing the WSN to sense the data. It exploits the benefits of WSNs and Passive infrared sensors. It reduces energy consumption by cutting out the avoidable wastage of energy. It is a scalable implementation as it can be easily scaled up to and can be applied to intelligent buildings [16].

6. Conservation of Cultural Heritage-Application of Energy conservation IoT

Cultural Heritage depicts the native cultures of countries and regions which are represented by either the artworks or linguistics or the architectural property. CH is to be preserved for the forthcoming generations because it carries the inherent properties, values, morals, and character from one generation to another. In case, it is damaged, it could cause severe loss which is not repairable. CH is a huge resource as it attracts tourists as per the artefacts and native culture. The motivation for selecting a specific destination for tourism is the inherent property in terms of the artistic and cultural heritage including the monuments, museums, exhibitions, historical villages, and others. The conservation of this artwork is a concern of special attention as time causes the natural deterioration of the heritage, against which CH is needed to be preserved. As time passes, external factors as climate, human, weather conditions, and internal causes like the material affect the heritage adversely and cause damage.

The goal is to implement the IoT concept with the intent of preserving the CH by providing a stable environment under the full control of administrative authorities. The controlled scenario is to be implemented with a smart home concept. It provides constant monitoring and regularization of the environment in which the heritage is preserved.

The stable atmospheres which are under full control are necessary for some buildings or monuments or artefacts that are prone to oxidation or which are light sensitive.

Such a controlled environment is established by appropriate monitoring and control of climate including temperature and moisture. Such a climate control system requires sensors to record the data about the climatic conditions and a microcontroller to control the conditions in the surroundings of the artefacts. This is achieved with the help of IoT architecture that helps to assess the damaging risks of artworks. Such kind of network arrangements are helpful for regular checks and the cumulative data record allows to predict and handle the accidents. The remote monitoring and online control protect and safeguard the artworks. It reduces the risk of vandalism, robbery, or accidental damages.

IoT dealing with CH involves the artwork as the “thing” to be preserved. The very first step is to install the sensor nodes to collect real-time data and gateways to transfer the data to the cloud. The entire setup allows online, real-time, constant monitoring and supervision of the specific CH. Cloud provides easy access to the collected and analyzed data, improving the safety of that artwork.

The collected information via sensors is worthy for curators and authorities to find out the most dangerous risk and their factors. After, locating the riskiest deteriorating situation, strategic planning can be made, and follow-up actions are implemented. UPV already launched a multidisciplinary project for preserving CH using IoT. It comprises experts from art restoration, climate monitoring, statistics, wireless communications, cloud engineers, etc [17-19].

For a specific project, following the requirement statement for conserving a particular CH, the very first step is to design the specific nodes for data transmission that must be capable to monitor the climate with minimum maintenance, as shown in Fig. (10). Designing the appropriate nodes in the IoT implementation is the most crucial issue. It covers minimizing the power consumption by considering the communication distances.

Fig. (10))
IoT design with sensors and gateways.
Fig. (11))
Sigfox implementation sample.

To date, no ideal usage has been made, yet the correlation with effectively actualized principles can be made. New usage can be improved by making a correlation with previously existing arrangements. Two marked names in these fields are-LoRa [20] and Sigfox [21].

LoRa and Sigfox are business plans for executing WAN [22], as shown in Fig. (11), with low force utilization working with sensors. An assortment of hubs is assessed in various conditions for saving the CH. Thinking about the assortment of fine arts, the tricky issues emerging with respect to the force flexibly and Internet associations are various. For instance, the showcase of craftsmanship might be in the gallery or might be in the city. Numerous destinations exist without power flexibly [23] (archeological exhuming, disconnected church, and so on), as shown in Fig. (12).

Fig. (12))
Preserving CH with different situations.

Conclusion and Future Scope

This chapter has assessed the force necessities of the hubs for a standard observing issue of air, temperature, and stickiness. The outcomes recommend that it is conceivable to make a hub with a life expectancy of over 10 years utilizing standard batteries. This work planned a suitable cloud framework to manage CH observing necessities. This work introduced IoT engineering for this reason. The necessities from the craftsmanship stance, information obtaining, Cloud preparing, and information perception to the end client are given. The outcomes introduced in this work centers around the most basic part of the engineering, which are the sensor hubs. An answer dependent on LoRa and Sigfox advances is conceived to create the base effect in the craftsmanship, accomplishing a life expectancy of over 10 years. The arrangement will be fit for scaling the preparing and capacity assets, sent either in a public or on-premise cloud, inserting complex prescient models. This blend of advancements can adapt to various kinds of social legacy conditions in the future. In the future, Machine learning and IoT [24, 25] can be blended to automate decision-making. Classification techniques [26, 27, 28] can be incorporated to extend this current project.

Blockchain: Concept and Emergence



Shazmeen Shamsi1, Mohd. M. Haque2, Sushil Kumar3, Jawed Ahmed4, Mohammad Sufian Badar4, 5, *
1 Department of Computer Science, Jamia Millia Islamia, New Delhi, India
2 School of Computer and Systems Sciences, Jawaharlal Nehru University, New Delhi, India
3 Department of Computer Science, Jamia Hamdard University, New Delhi, India
4 Department of Bioengineering, University of California, Riverside, CA, USA
5 Salfia Paramedical Institute Darbhanga, SPI Darbhanga, Bihar 846001, India

Abstract

Artificial Intelligence (AI), Machine Learning (ML), and Internet of Things (IoT) are incredible instances of the technology paradigm and have brought about revolutionary changes in the communication, internet, and digital sectors coalesced into one and induced a landslide shift in the lifestyle of mankind. But the privacy concerns of the individuals can be trustfully and transparently addressed only by the use of ingenious contemporary technology, the Blockchain. Blockchain is a kind of distributed ledger technology that uses a peer-to-peer, distributed network to make a continuous, growing chain of time-stamped records to form a digital ledger. It is decentralized, which means that it can not be controlled by a central authority. It has numerous advantages like transparency, immutability, decentralization and can be incorporated in almost any sector of the society to increase the security of the transactions being carried out. This chapter predominantly focuses on the concepts of Blockchain and aims to do a comprehensive survey on its advantages and applications and also highlights the future prospects because even though it is so popular today, this technology is still in its infancy.

Keywords: AI, Artificial Intelligence, Blockchain, Decentralization, Distributed Ledger Technology, Immutability, Internet of Things, IoT, ML, Machine learning, Transparency.

1. INTRODUCTION

“The blockchain revolution has a greater potential than anything we’ve seen in history. It’s bigger than the Internet revolution, how it’s going to restructure society.” —Patrick Byrne

The above remark made by Patrick Byrne, CEO and founder of Overstock.com, is as apt as it can be. Today, Blockchain technology is taking the globe by storm. The enormous amount of buzz and hype that this technology has created is attracting the attention of a massive number of researchers and scientists and will eventually create the dawn of a new revolution. This chapter aims to provide a brief but clear understanding of the important aspects of this technology among the readers.

The ultimate objective of mankind is the absolute modernization of his lifestyle for attaining the highest levels of comfort. This can practically be achieved only with the development and evolution of science and technology, which eventually gives us a plethora of immensely useful applications that make life easy for us. We are currently living in an era where the world is changing at a rapid pace, and there is a constant shift of the common mass towards contemporary technologies. These innovations have affected every industry, be it business, healthcare, energy, financial markets, and so on, to the extent that in recent times, these technologies have reached the comfort of our homes and affected the way we live and socialize [1]. In this world powered by technology, everything comes at a cost We are living in times where we are experiencing a gradual transition from cash to cashless payments. Electronic Cash or E-Cash, the digital form of cash, is allowing easy and hassle-free transactions of money over the internet. This invention is in and of itself a commendable achievement that has caused a stir in the world of trade and commerce, and the immense importance of which can be understood during pandemics like Covid-19. But cybercrooks, with their felonious talents, are ever-ready to make some profit by breaching data of the transactions being carried out. Secure online transactions that involve third parties also cannot always be trusted. Let alone cybercriminals and private third parties, it has often been seen that the economic growth and development of most of the developing countries is often impeded by corrupt government officials who misuse their authority to satisfy their greed for monetary gains. Blockchain technology, which first came into the picture with the advent of Bitcoin in 2008 [2], promises decentralized and secure transactions over the internet and is a successful attempt to solve these problems.

Blockchain is a magical technology that can be clubbed with almost all other technologies available today to add to their benefits and make them safe and secure for use. It is a Distributed Ledger Technology that records and maintains transactions chronologically, occurring on a peer-to-peer scale where every peer essentially maintains a copy of the transactions being carried out. An important point to be noted is that ‘no third party is involved’ and validation of new blocks to be added to the chain occurs only if at least 51% of the participants vote in favor, otherwise it is rejected. Now you must be wondering, what are blocks? They can simply be understood as pages of a record book that constantly register the latest transaction being carried out. Once a block runs out of memory (think of it as a page being filled with records), a new block is needed to be generated for which voting is done for its final validation and generation.

Blockchain is a revolutionary technology that came into practical existence with the advent of the cryptocurrency, Bitcoins, which caused a lot of excitement among the people and has intrigued researchers to study it thoroughly. Learning about it would help us remain updated with the most demanding skills of today. The true potential of this revolutionary technology needs to be understood by all so that we can make its best use. It is expected to re-shape the industrial trends and cause a transition from paper-based/non-secure online transactions to secure, privacy-preserving, immutable e-transactions.

This chapter will give you a basic idea of Blockchain Technology. Each topic is explained in a detailed but crisp manner. The authors have tried to make the chapter more interesting by incorporating a hands-on activity about the deployment of smart contracts to attract the interest in this topic of the readers. It is hoped to generate a clear understanding among the readers about this incredible technology which is way ahead of its time.

In the following chapter, we will first study how the need for this technology arose, followed by its history. We will then be introduced to the different elements that are essentially a part of every blockchain network. We will then learn about its features owing to which this technology has become immensely important in today’s world. Next, we will study the types, working, applications, advantages, and challenges related to this topic.

2. THE NEED FOR BLOCKCHAIN TECHNOLOGY

The need for this technology arose with the International Banking Crisis which led to the collapse of investment in 2008. Bitcoin cryptocurrency, which was the first practical example of this technology, brings along with it many advantages such as efficiency, cost-effectiveness, reliability, and a privacy-preserving secure system for creating and recording financial transactions. With an ever-increasing human population and urbanization, the number of transactions being carried out is increasing exponentially and is speculated to affect the complexity, efficiency, and cost of the current systems and in turn, increase its vulnerability [3]. The current digital transactions rely on trusted third parties to fulfill their objectives. Whether these parties can be trusted or not is a big question. Though these third parties which can be anyone- a Government Agency or a private owner- claim that the transactions are carried out in a secure environment, is it so? Studies have shown that even though a few central authorities follow strict protocols to preserve the privacy and security of transactions to the extent that they provide certificates of assurance, these technologies are not foolproof. For example, SSL certificates that ensure us that we are on a secure website have also been stolen from few very well-known domains of Microsoft, Twitter, Facebook, and Central Intelligence Agency (CIA), UK.

Also, in today’s world where everything is done at the click of a mouse, we need a technology that is efficient, fast, and at the same time affordable. Blockchain is the perfect solution to this. People don’t need to pay money to any third party, like a bank or a private organization, to maintain their records as digital ledgers which can be monitored with ease and precision owing to the peer-to-peer, distributed, and decentralized nature of the Blockchain architecture. The world needs a technology like Blockchain which provides a safe and secure platform for carrying out the transactions, complete ownership and authority over one’s property, and also transparent transactions to be carried out. The data once stored on the network cannot be changed or deleted because of its immutability. This is an immensely important feature that can be used to store extremely important and private data on the system. This technology also addresses another very important issue- the problem of Double-Spending. Since the records are maintained strictly chronologically, one can easily keep a track of the money spent and avoid the problem and loss caused by double-spending [4]. Having understood its need, let us now learn about this technology in detail.

3. HISTORY AND EVOLUTION

This topic first came into existence on 31st October 2008, when Satoshi Nakamoto’s white paper “Bitcoin: A Peer-to-Peer Electronic Cash System” was released [2]. The paper discussed a “peer-to-peer version of electronic cash”, which allowed online payments to be sent directly from one party to the other without involving a bank or any other financial institution. This discussion is incomplete without mentioning Bitcoins. Bitcoin is the first practical example of Blockchain technology, that came into existence in 2009 and is the first-ever cryptocurrency to be developed and is the most popular example of this technology. It is an innovative method of payment that has introduced virtual cash and is one of its kind. Considering its design, it only allows the exchange and distribution of digital information and bars its modification. You must remember that Blockchain and Bitcoin are not the same things. Blockchain is the technology used for the creation of the digital currency, Bitcoin. Let us understand this with the help of an example. A mobile phone is just a small metallic object without an Operating System (OS). OS adds life to the phone and is simply software that manages the tasks and its worth can only be understood by the applications it can host. Considering this example, a Blockchain is analogous to the Operating System and Bitcoins to the applications it holds.

To date, three generations of Blockchain have emerged and the fourth one is under evolution. Let’s quickly discuss the generations in brief:

3.1. First Generation/ Blockchain 1.0

They were simple ledgers that recorded transactions and were built to improve the existing monetary system. Examples of first-generation cryptocurrencies include Bitcoin, Litecoin, etc. All these cryptocurrencies are written using C++ and consider the Bitcoin source code as their basis. Also, they use the Proof-of-Work Mechanism which makes these networks pretty costly because the mining involved here requires a lot of electricity.

3.2. Second Generation/ Blockchain 2.0

It emerged in 2015 with the advent of Ethereum which has much wider functionalities. The most important feature that was added in this generation is the Smart Contracts that make the transactions secure by allowing the automatic execution of all conditions. It is written using Solidity programming language and suffers from the problem of scalability.

3.3. Third Generation/ Blockchain 3.0

It comes with more advantages, like better design and wider functionality, and has tried to address the issue of scalability. Some examples are Cardano, Nano, etc. Instead of Proof-of-Work, it incorporates the Proof-Of-Stake consensus mechanism.

3.4. Fourth Generation/ Blockchain 4.0

It has yet to come into existence but it is expected that this generation will evolve when Artificial Intelligence will meet this technology. Keeping in mind the fast pace of research and the growing interest of scientists, it seems that that day is not far when Blockchain 4.0 will become a reality.

4. PREFATORY ELEMENTS

This section introduces you to the generic elements of Blockchain technology.

4.1. Transaction

A transaction can be defined as an agreement between a supplier and a consumer that there will be an exchange of assets either in the present or in the future. It is the fundamental unit of Blockchain and represents exchange by transfer of values from one participant to another [5]. A transaction includes the recipient, signature identifying the sender, amount of cryptocurrency being used for the transaction to be brought into action, gas price (cost paid by the sender per computational step), and the gas limit (maximum number of steps a transaction is supposed to be executed in). In technical terms, the transaction can be defined as the product of gas price and the amount of gas used.

4.2. Node

The term node refers to the user or participant who is a part of the Blockchain platform. They form the building blocks of the Blockchain Infrastructure. When an attempt to add a new block to the chain is made, it is reflected on all the nodes in the system, and depending upon how authentic a transaction is, the nodes can together accept or reject the proposal. Nodes can either be online or offline. Online nodes are active participants in the network while offline modes need to catch up with the latest advances by downloading the new blocks that were added when they were in offline conditions. The process of updating the offline blocks is known as synchronization.

4.3. Mining

It is the process of adding transactions to the distributed public ledger technology, blockchain, and it is this process that facilitates the safe exchange of assets in the network. Miners are the nodes that are capable of mining. They have to solve a cryptographic puzzle for the creation of valid blocks and the miner who solves the puzzle first gets to add a block to the networked chain. They broadcast their block on the network which then requires verification from all the nodes in the network irrespective of whether they are simple nodes or miners. If the block is voted to be valid following the consensus mechanisms (which is explained in Section 4.7 of this Chapter), the block is added to the chain, otherwise, it is rejected.

4.4. Block

A ‘block’ can simply be defined as a collection of transactions that are bundled together logically. It is these blocks that are chained together to form Blockchain technology.

Structurally, a block consists of a block header and a block body [6]. The block header includes the Block Version, Merkle Tree Root Hash, Hash of parent block, Time Stamp, nBits, Nonce (Number used only once), and the Block Body includes all the transactions carried out in that block. Block Header includes the block metadata which can be visualized in Fig. (1) and better understood from the list that follows:

Fig. (1))
Structure of a Block.
  • Block version – It refers to the current version of the block structure.
  • Merkle Tree Root Hash - A cryptographic hash of all of the transactions that a block contains.
  • Hash of parent block – The hash value of the preceding/parent block, which is always linked to the succeeding/child block.
  • Time Stamp - The exact time at which a block was created.
  • nBits – It refers to the target, i.e., the threshold value below which a block header hash must be for a block to be valid.
  • Nonce (“number used once”) - A random value that is added to a hashed block which, when re-hashed, matched the difficult restrictions.

Each block in the Blockchain is linked to two other blocks- one, its parent block, and two, its child block. It is to be remembered that there can only be one parent to a child block and a single child to a parent block as shown in Fig. (2).

Fig. (2))
How different blocks are linked together.

The first block to be added to a Blockchain network is known as “Genesis Block”.

Types of Blocks:

  • Main branch Blocks- They extend the longest chain in the network, i.e., the main blockchain.
  • Side Branch Blocks- Though they are currently not present practically, they are expected to be incorporated as a part of the main branch in a side chain soon, due to the growth of transaction rate.
  • Orphan Blocks- Those blocks that point to a parent block that is unknown to the node processing it.

4.5. Hashing

Hashing is the process by which input of simple integers and strings is converted into a complicated, non-decipherable encrypted output using complex mathematical algorithms. Hashes of any length of input always give a fixed length of output and this makes it practically impossible to guess the original word. It is a ‘one-way function’ and can not be cracked. So how does it help in checking the authenticity of a message? Let’s understand this with the help of an example. Suppose you want to download some important official document that is highly confidential. For this purpose, you will need a hash of the document and the digital signature of the sender which in turn is also hashed. The document message is encrypted and made it impossible to decipher using hashing algorithms. They act as a trap-door function that prevents it from being traced. The receiver decrypts the message using the same hashing function and signature and compares the two hash values. If the same hash value is produced, it can be said that the document and signature are both authentic. There are many algorithms available for this purpose, like different versions of the Secure Hashing Algorithm (SHA) – SHA1, SHA 224, SHA256, SHA512, etc., Message Digest Algorithm (MD) – MD2, MD4, MD5, Keccak – Keccak-256, Keccak 512, etc., Shake(Secure Hash Algorithm and KECCAK) with versions 128 and 256. Shake is different in the sense that it produces any length of the output.

Let us see how each of these algorithms produces different outputs for the same word. Suppose the word is “Abracadabra”, the following Table 1 shows its corresponding hash value using a few of the many different algorithms available.

Table 1 Showing the different hash values produced using different hashing algorithms for the same message.
Algorithm Used Message hash for “Abracadabra”
SHA256 46abe7c8d7f5033272383a4a2483ca72ca35c6bf7d8b4b9d60eee94fd7aebce2
SHA512 e31635ff8390c8df27ea5ecd810a73c92ba62a399fafe52b78467abf21d9e4adaf833
31238905f491bdcc1d7bfddf4d89074b4599638d4504c4eedfde45c62a9
MD5 53a5e9533a3a2dd2685f4f112b82379a
Keccak-256 cdbeda58b615b3bbdc510ac7b6aa14e618709a6c994e062e5e34e37e5992ab7a
Shake-256 abb7c5ab0ddcc178933e95cd40466fd2f6f7190ad1bdcb72e59e8fe447972f94da72
7056749cc535d94fead6a0ba697184427fd1fcf31ed5ef65e927ef943d00

The hash value for two messages will always be different. Therefore, hashing safeguards the privacy of the participants by ensuring secure transmission of the message intended for a particular recipient only.

4.6. Digital Signatures

The data to be transacted has to be secured using digital signatures which ensure that the data is not meddled with. The purpose of using digital signatures is to bind the signatory to the message which is to be sent. For this purpose, the participants use cryptography techniques which may either be public-key cryptography or private key cryptography. Digital signatures are the digital counterpart of handwritten signatures and should ideally be non-forgeable by any attacker. Let us gain an understanding of how digital signatures work. To sign a document, the sender uses two mutually authenticating keys, that is, the sender’s private key and the receiver’s public key. The mathematical algorithm used acts like a cipher, by encrypting the data and creating a hash of the message. Only the correct receiver can decode the document by using his private key and the sender’s public key. If in any case the document changes after signing, the digital signature becomes invalid. There are several digital signature algorithms available.

Following Fig. (3) shows a flowchart of secure data transmission in the Blockchain platform.

4.7. Consensus

It is an important part of the blockchain architecture and is used for the verification and validation of transactions and blocks in the Blockchain is known as Consensus. It follows a set of self-enforced rules which every participant in the network has to abide by and thus helps in maintaining the integrity and synchronization of the nodes that are a part of this chain [7]. It also ensures that an identical and updated set of copies of the transactions is distributed among the participants. Its advantage is that it provides security, privacy, and transparency in the network. Some consensus algorithms are mentioned below:

  • Proof-of-Work (POW)- The core idea behind this algorithm is to solve a complex mathematical puzzle that easily gives out a solution. The mathematical problem, in reality, uses a lot of computational power. The solution to the problem should be a number less than the hash of the block and the miner who solves the puzzle first is given some reward in terms of cryptocurrency. Ethereum, Litecoin, Dogecoin are few cryptocurrencies that use this algorithm.
  • Proof-of-Stake (POS)- The essence of this algorithm is the same as a POW, but the approach is different. Here, there are ‘validators’ instead of miners who bet on the blocks they think will be added to the chain. Based on which block is added to the chair, their reward is proportionate to their investment and their stakes increase in value.
  • Delegated Proof of Stake (DPoS)- The main idea behind this is that participants stake a few of their coins and elect delegates on their behalf. The weightage given to each delegate depends upon the investment made. It is thus one of the fastest consensus algorithms.
  • Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (PBFT)- The basic idea behind this algorithm is that, depending upon the status of the ledger, the algorithm works even in the presence of a few faulty nodes or when a few nodes fail to respond. Hyperledger Fabric, Tendermint, Zilliqa are few platforms that use this algorithm [8, 9].
  • Proof of Activity (PoA)- This is an energy-efficient and fast algorithm which uses a hybrid approach by clubbing the PoW and PoS algorithms. Here, validators or verified accounts are entrusted with the task of ensuring the correctness of the block.
  • Proof of Capacity (PoC)- It is also called Proof of Space and is similar to the Proof of Work algorithm, where the computation of PoW is analogous to storage in PoC. The solution to the complex mathematical problems is accumulated in the storage used to produce blocks and the fastest one in computing the solution stands a better chance of creating blocks. The process which this algorithm follows is known as ‘plotting’.
  • Proof of Burn (PoB)- In this algorithm, instead of taking money, the participants burn their coins/ cryptocurrency by sending it to an address from where it is irretrievable. The greater the amount of cryptocurrency they burn, the greater is their chance of being selected for the next block.
  • Proof of Elapsed Time (PoET)- It works by generating a random sleep time for each participant during which the particular nodes go into sleep mode. If a block is available when a particular node wakes up or becomes active, that participant is considered the lucky winner and gets a chance to commit a new block to the Blockchain.
Fig. (3))
How does Blockchain work?
